<a id="pm-s1"></a>
## 1. Abstract and result contract

> We prove a directional zero–one law for uniformly elliptic nearest-neighbor random walks in stationary, ergodic, finite-range-dependent environments on ℤ<sup><i>d</i></sup>, <i>d</i> ≥ 3. For every fixed nonzero real direction, the probability of escape in that direction, averaged over the environment, is either zero or one. Finite-range dependence is imposed on the full transition rows: collections of rows at distance greater than a fixed range are independent, including collections indexed by infinite deterministic sets.

- **C1** (theorem, [source](#stmt-thm-main)): Under assumptions <span class="upright">(i)–(iii)</span>, for every fixed $\ell\in\mathbb R^d\setminus\{0\}$, $$P_0(A_\ell)\in\{0,1\}.$$
  - Explanation: For every fixed nonzero direction, the annealed escape probability is either zero or one under the stated environment assumptions.
- **C2** (conditional_result, [source](#stmt-lem-separated-weights)): Let $\gamma$ be a fixed finite marked word, and let $B\subseteq\mathbb Z^d$ be a deterministic set. With the convention that distance from an empty set is infinite, suppose that $$\mathop{\mathrm{dist}}_\infty(\operatorname{Act}(\gamma),B)>R.$$ If $f\ge0$ is measurable with respect to the rows in $B$, then $$\label{eq:separated-weights} E_Q[p_\omega(\gamma)f(\omega)] =W(\gamma)E_Q[f(\omega)].$$ The conclusion also holds when the active set is empty, without a separation requirement.* *For a deterministic set $F\subseteq\mathbb Z^d$, the quenched law of the marked walk stopped on its first arrival in $F$, including the arrival site, depends only on rows outside $F$. In particular, the quenched probability of any measurable infinite-path event that implies the walk never enters $F$ depends only on those rows. Such probabilities may therefore be used as $f$ in <a href="#eq:separated-weights" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:separated-weights">[eq:separated-weights]</a> whenever the exterior rows are separated from $\operatorname{Act}(\gamma)$.
  - Explanation: A marked path reveals only its active departure rows. Separation of those rows permits exact factorization, including stopped and surviving path laws.
- **C3** (conditional_result, [source](#stmt-prop-cut-law)): Fix a normalized real direction $v$ and suppose $p_v>0$.* 1. *Under the raw law $P_0$, on $\{\sup_n h(X_n)=\infty\}$ there is a cut almost surely.* 2. *Under $\widehat P_v$, there are infinitely many cuts. With time zero counted as an initial boundary, the relative marked words between successive boundaries are independent and identically distributed. Their common law $\nu_v$ assigns mass $$\label{eq:regeneration-word-law} \nu_v(\{\gamma\})=W(\gamma)$$ to each regeneration word $\gamma$. These finite words concatenate to the entire conditioned walk.* 3. *Under the raw law, conditional on any realized prefix through the first cut, the translated suffix has law $\widehat P_v$. This assertion is restricted to the event that a cut exists.
  - Explanation: Conditioning on nonbacktracking produces independent regeneration words; the raw suffix law is identified by finite prefixes rather than a Markov property at a future-selected cut.
- **C4** (conditional_result, [source](#stmt-prop-sign-union)): For every fixed $v\ne0$, $$\label{eq:sign-union} P_0(A_v)>0\quad\Longrightarrow\quad p_v>0\ \text{ and }\ P_0(A_v\cup A_{-v})=1.$$ More precisely, when $p_v>0$, infinite height supremum implies $A_v$ almost surely, and finite height supremum implies $A_{-v}$ almost surely.
  - Explanation: Positive directional escape makes the two opposite signs exhaustive and implies positive nonbacktracking probability.
- **C5** (bound, [source](#stmt-prop-mean-width)): For every normalized real direction $v$ with $p_v>0$, the regeneration law of Proposition <a href="#prop:cut-law" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:cut-law">3</a> satisfies $$\label{eq:finite-mean-width} E_{\nu_v}L<\infty.$$
  - Explanation: Regeneration words have finite mean spatial width, without a time moment assumption.
- **C6** (bound, [source](#stmt-lem-stationary-maximal)): Let $(Z_i)_{i\ge1}$ be a stationary sequence of nonnegative integrable random variables. For every integer $n\ge1$ and every $a>0$, $$\mathbb P\left\{\max_{1\le k\le n}\frac1k\sum_{i=1}^k Z_i>a\right\} \le \frac{\mathbb EZ_1}{a}.$$
  - Explanation: Stationarity and integrability control the largest initial running average through a maximal inequality.
- **C7** (bound, [source](#stmt-prop-radius-moment)): Let $v\ne0$ satisfy $\\|v\\|_\infty=1$. If $P_0(A_v)>0$ and $P_0(A_{-v})>0$, then the regeneration-word laws of Proposition <a href="#prop:cut-law" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:cut-law">3</a> satisfy $$E_{\nu_v}\mathcal R+E_{\nu_{-v}}\mathcal R<\infty. \label{eq:radius-moment}$$
  - Explanation: Hypothetical coexistence forces finite mean spatial radius for both regeneration laws.
- **C8** (conditional_result, [source](#stmt-lem-opposite-rays)): Under the coexistence hypotheses of Proposition <a href="#prop:radius-moment" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:radius-moment">7</a>, there are deterministic unit vectors $r_+,r_-$ such that, for $\sigma\in\{+,-\}$, $$\|X_n\|\longrightarrow\infty,\qquad \frac{X_n}{\|X_n\|}\longrightarrow r_\sigma \quad P_0\text{-almost surely on }A_{\sigma v}.$$ They satisfy $r_\sigma\cdot(\sigma v)>0$ and $r_-=-r_+$.
  - Explanation: Under hypothetical coexistence, the two directional escape events have deterministic opposite limiting rays.
- **C9** (conditional_result, [source](#stmt-prop-coordinate-reduction)): If $0<P_0(A_v)<1$ for some fixed nonzero real direction $v$, then there is a coordinate $i\in\{1,\ldots,d\}$ such that $$P_0(A_{e_i})>0\qquad\text{and}\qquad P_0(A_{-e_i})>0.$$
  - Explanation: An intermediate directional escape probability would imply coexistence in a coordinate direction.
- **C10** (conditional_result, [source](#stmt-prop-exact-cut-bridges)): The renewal masses satisfy $$\bar u_0^\sigma=1,\qquad \bar u_j^\sigma=0\quad(1\le j<M),\qquad \bar u_H^\sigma=\xi u_{H-M}^\sigma\quad(H\ge M). \label{eq:bridge-renewal}$$ For a list of slabs $(\gamma_1,\ldots,\gamma_m)$ with total width $H$, its mass under $\mathcal B_H^\sigma$ is $$\mathcal B_H^\sigma(\gamma_1,\ldots,\gamma_m) =\frac{\prod_{i=1}^m W(\gamma_i)}{\bar u_H^\sigma}. \label{eq:bridge-law}$$ In particular, reversing the list order preserves this law; the steps within each word retain their original order.* *For $H\ge M$, concatenating the list gives the raw marked path through its first hit of $H$, conditioned on $\mathcal D_H^\sigma$. Each such path has a unique parsing into slabs. Concatenating any prescribed slab words, or successive bridges of prescribed widths, gives a raw path weight equal to the product of the constituent raw weights.
  - Explanation: Exact-cut bridges have normalized product weights and preserve their law when slab lists are reversed while each word is traversed forward.
- **C11** (bound, [source](#stmt-lem-width-tail)): For either sign and all integers $n'\ge n\ge0$, $$u_n^\sigma-u_{n'}^\sigma \le C(n'-n)F_\sigma(n), \qquad \sum_{l\ge0}2^lF_\sigma(2^l)<\infty. \label{eq:width-tail-comparison}$$
  - Explanation: Finite mean width yields a dyadically summable tail and controls differences of hitting probabilities.
- **C12** (conditional_result, [source](#stmt-prop-common-blocks)): The common blocks exist indefinitely, and their pairs of slab lists are iid. Their widths $K_i$ have a common law satisfying $$M\le K_i<\infty\quad\text{a.s.}, \qquad \mu:=\mathbf E K_i<\infty.$$ The expected sum of the radii of all slabs on both sides of one common block is finite. In particular, if $$W'_0=0,\qquad W'_i=\sum_{j=1}^iK_j, \label{eq:common-width-sums}$$ then $W'_i/i\to\mu$ almost surely.
  - Explanation: Independent opposed renewal tapes have independent common blocks with finite mean width and integrable sums of slab radii.
- **C13** (bound, [source](#stmt-prop-entropy-comparison)): For every integer $n\ge1$, the actual chunk law and the reference law above satisfy $$D(P^{(n)}\Vert Q^{(n)})\le C+h_{14n}'-h_n'. \label{eq:entropy-comparison}$$
  - Explanation: The relative entropy cost of the actual chunk placement is controlled by the growth of lateral-displacement entropy.
- **C14** (bound, [source](#stmt-prop-reference-contact)): For every integer $n\ge1$, $$Q^{(n)}(E_{\rm mid})\le\delta_n, \qquad \delta_n=\min\{1,Cn(F_+(n)+F_-(n))\}. \label{eq:reference-contact}$$
  - Explanation: Independent bridge reference laws bound middle-chunk proximity contacts by the width tails.
- **C15** (bound, [source](#stmt-prop-contact-upper)): For the two independent opposed tapes under coordinate coexistence, the contact count defined in <a href="#eq:contact-count" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:contact-count">[eq:contact-count]</a> satisfies $$m(J)=O\!\left(\frac{J}{\log J}\right) \qquad(J\to\infty). \label{eq:contact-upper}$$
  - Explanation: Entropy comparison gives an upper bound on the number of dyadic block-index intervals containing contacts under hypothetical coordinate coexistence.
- **C16** (bound, [source](#stmt-lem-posterior-max)): Let $m\ge1$, and let $(w_i(e))_{i\ge0}$, $1\le e\le m$, be nonnegative martingales for the same filtration $(\mathcal F_i)_{i\ge0}$, with $\sum_{e=1}^m w_i(e)\le1$ almost surely for every $i$. Set $$A_i(e)=\max_{0\le j\le i}w_j(e),\qquad A_i=\sum_{e=1}^m A_i(e),\qquad A_\infty=\lim_{i\to\infty}A_i, \qquad B=\log(m+1).$$ Write also $A_\infty(e)=\lim_i A_i(e)$. There are absolute constants $c,C>0$ such that $$\label{eq:posterior-exponential} \mathbb E\exp(cA_\infty/B)\le C.$$ Moreover, on any joint probability space with this martingale marginal, every event $V$ of probability $p$ satisfies $$\label{eq:event-weighted-max} \mathbb E[A_\infty\mathbf 1_V] \le CBp\log(\mathrm e/p),$$ where the right-hand side is defined to be zero at $p=0$. The event $V$ need not belong to the martingale filtration.
  - Explanation: A finite family of nonnegative martingales has an exponentially integrable sum of running maxima on a logarithmic scale, including bounds restricted to arbitrary events.
- **C17** (bound, [source](#stmt-prop-first-contact)): Assume $p_+p_->0$, and let $M\le k<r<N$ be integers with $r-k\ge R$. Start a positive bridge $Y$ with law $\mathcal B_N^+$ at the origin, and put $E_{\mathrm{lat}}=\pi Y_{T_N}$. Independently of $Y$, sample a relative marked path with law $\widehat P_-$, and translate it to start at $(N-1,E_{\mathrm{lat}})$; denote the resulting path by $\beta$. Define $$\tau=\inf\{i:T_k(Y)\le i<T_N(Y),\quad \\|Y_i-\beta_j\\|_\infty\le R\text{ for some }j\ge0\},$$ with $\inf\varnothing=\infty$. Let $$\label{eq:first-contact-event} \mathcal C_{k,r,N}= \left\{ \begin{gathered} \mathcal D_k^+(Y),\quad \tau<T_N(Y),\quad (Y_\tau)_1\ge r,\\ \min_{T_k(Y)\le i\le\tau}(Y_i)_1\ge k, \quad \|E_{\mathrm{lat}}\|\le N^2 \end{gathered} \right\}.$$ Here $\mathcal D_k^+(Y)$ is a condition on the prefix through the first hit of $k$. There is a constant $C$, independent of $k,r,N$, such that, with $\Delta=u_{r-k}^+-u_{N-k}^+$, $$\label{eq:first-contact-bound} P(\mathcal C_{k,r,N}) \le C\log(2N)\,\Delta\log^2(\mathrm e/\Delta).$$ The right-hand side is defined to be zero when $\Delta=0$.
  - Explanation: A scripted prefix and endpoint posterior estimates bound the probability of a late first proximity contact.
- **C18** (bound, [source](#stmt-prop-contact-lower)): Suppose that $P_0(A_{e_1})>0$ and $P_0(A_{-e_1})>0$, and let $m(J)$ be the contact statistic of the opposed tapes defined in <a href="#eq:contact-count" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:contact-count">[eq:contact-count]</a>. There are constants $c>0$ and an integer $c_1\ge0$ such that, for all sufficiently large integers $J$, $$\label{eq:contact-lower} m(J+c_1)\ge \frac{cJ}{1+\log\log J}.$$
  - Explanation: Under hypothetical coordinate coexistence, first-contact estimates force a lower contact count incompatible with the entropy upper bound.

        "anchor": "stmt-prop-exact-cut-bridges",
        "exact_quote": "The renewal masses satisfy $$\\bar u_0^\\sigma=1,\\qquad\n \\bar u_j^\\sigma=0\\quad(1\\le j<M),\\qquad\n \\bar u_H^\\sigma=\\xi u_{H-M}^\\sigma\\quad(H\\ge M).\n \\label{eq:bridge-renewal}$$ For a list of slabs $(\\gamma_1,\\ldots,\\gamma_m)$ with total width $H$, its mass under $\\mathcal B_H^\\sigma$ is $$\\mathcal B_H^\\sigma(\\gamma_1,\\ldots,\\gamma_m)\n   =\\frac{\\prod_{i=1}^m W(\\gamma_i)}{\\bar u_H^\\sigma}.\n \\label{eq:bridge-law}$$ In particular, reversing the list order preserves this law; the steps within each word retain their original order.*\n\n*For $H\\ge M$, concatenating the list gives the raw marked path through its first hit of $H$, conditioned on $\\mathcal D_H^\\sigma$.         "anchor": "stmt-lem-posterior-max",
        "exact_quote": "Let $m\\ge1$, and let $(w_i(e))_{i\\ge0}$, $1\\le e\\le m$, be nonnegative martingales for the same filtration $(\\mathcal F_i)_{i\\ge0}$, with $\\sum_{e=1}^m w_i(e)\\le1$ almost surely for every $i$. Set $$A_i(e)=\\max_{0\\le j\\le i}w_j(e),\\qquad\n A_i=\\sum_{e=1}^m A_i(e),\\qquad\n A_\\infty=\\lim_{i\\to\\infty}A_i,\n \\qquad B=\\log(m+1).$$ Write also $A_\\infty(e)=\\lim_i A_i(e)$. There are absolute constants $c,C>0$ such that $$\\label{eq:posterior-exponential}\n \\mathbb E\\exp(cA_\\infty/B)\\le C.$$ Moreover, on any joint probability space with this martingale marginal, every event $V$ of probability $p$ satisfies $$\\label{eq:event-weighted-max}\n \\mathbb E[A_\\infty\\mathbf 1_V]\n \\le CBp\\log(\\mathrm e/p),$$ where the right-hand side is defined to be zero at $p=0$. The event $V$ need not belong to the martingale filtration.",
        "source_id": "PM-220.1"
      },
      "evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "stmt-lem-posterior-max",
          "exact_quote": "Let $m\\ge1$, and let $(w_i(e))_{i\\ge0}$, $1\\le e\\le m$, be nonnegative martingales for the same filtration $(\\mathcal F_i)_{i\\ge0}$, with $\\sum_{e=1}^m w_i(e)\\le1$ almost surely for every $i$. There are absolute constants $c,C>0$ such that $$\\label{eq:posterior-exponential}\n \\mathbb E\\exp(cA_\\infty/B)\\le C.$$ Moreover, on any joint probability space with this martingale marginal, every event $V$ of probability $p$ satisfies $$\\label{eq:event-weighted-max}\n \\mathbb E[A_\\infty\\mathbf 1_V]\n \\le CBp\\log(\\mathrm e/p),$$ where the right-hand side is defined to be zero at $p=0$.           "source_id": "PM-220.1"

<!-- PM:END pm.claims.v2 -->

<a id="pm-s2"></a>
## 2. Definitions, validity and bounds

- **environment row** ([source](#pm-h-3)): An environment $\omega\in\Omega$ assigns the row $\omega(x,\cdot)$ to each site $x$.
- **annealed law** ([source](#pm-h-3)): Its annealed law is $P_x=\int P_{x,\omega}\,Q(d\omega)$.
- **directional escape** ([source](#pm-h-3)): For each fixed $\ell\in\mathbb R^d\setminus\{0\}$, write $$A_\ell=\{X_n\cdot\ell\longrightarrow+\infty\}.$$
- **marked word** ([source](#pm-h-7)): A finite marked word $\gamma$ consists of positions $x_0,\ldots,x_t$, with $x_{j+1}-x_j\in U$, and one mark for each of its $t$ steps.
- **departure sets** ([source](#pm-h-7)): Its departure set and active departure set are $$\mathop{\mathrm{Dep}}(\gamma)=\{x_0,\ldots,x_{t-1}\},\qquad \operatorname{Act}(\gamma) =\{x_j:0\le j<t,\ \text{step }j\text{ has mark }*\}.$$
- **annealed word weight** ([source](#pm-h-7)): Let $p_\omega(\gamma)$ be the product of the marked transition probabilities in <a href="#eq:marked-kernel" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:marked-kernel">[eq:marked-kernel]</a>, and put $$W(\gamma)=E_Q[p_\omega(\gamma)].$$
- **height and script length** ([source](#pm-h-8)): Fix a direction $v\ne0$, rescale it so that $\lVert v\rVert_\infty=1$, and write $h(x)=v\cdot x$. Choose a coordinate step $a_v\in U$ with $h(a_v)=1$. Fix an integer $M>dR+1$, the same integer for every direction considered, and set $\xi=\lambda^M$.
- **nonbacktracking law** ([source](#pm-h-8)): Write $$D_v=\{h(X_n)\ge0\text{ for every }n\ge0\},\qquad p_v=P_0(D_v),\qquad \widehat P_v=P_0(\,\cdot\mid D_v)\quad(p_v>0).$$
- **spatial radius** ([source](#sec:radius)): For a regeneration word $\gamma$ with position sequence $(x_0,\ldots,x_m)$, written relative to $x_0=0$, define its radius by $$\mathcal R(\gamma)=\max_{0\le j\le m}\|x_j\|,$$ where $\|\cdot\|$ denotes Euclidean norm.
- **width tail** ([source](#sec:bridges)): For integers $n\ge0$, set $$u_n^\sigma=P_0(T_n^\sigma<T_{-1}^\sigma), \qquad F_\sigma(n)=\nu_\sigma(L>n). \label{eq:hitting-width-tail}$$
- **contact** ([source](#pm-h-19)): A *contact* is a positive departure site at infinity distance at most $R$ from some negative departure site.
- **contact statistic** ([source](#eq:contact-count)): The quantity on which the two remaining estimates will disagree is $$m(J)=\sum_{j=1}^J\mathbf P\bigl\{ \text{a contact occurs in a positive block of index in } (2^j,2^{j+1}]\bigr\},\qquad J\ge1. \label{eq:contact-count}$$
- **regeneration word** ([source](#pm-h-8)): To describe the pieces between cuts, call a finite marked word $\gamma$ from zero a *regeneration word* if it has the following properties: 1. all its heights are nonnegative; 2. it ends at a candidate; 3. every earlier candidate in the word is undercut later within the word, meaning that a subsequent height is strictly smaller than that candidate’s height.
- **word width** ([source](#pm-h-8)): Its *width* $L(\gamma)$ is its terminal height.
- **cut** ([source](#pm-h-8)): A candidate time $t$ is a *cut* if $$h(X_n)\ge h(X_t)\qquad(n\ge t).$$
- **exact-cut bridge law** ([source](#pm-h-18)): If $\bar u_H^\sigma>0$, let $\mathcal B_H^\sigma$ denote the law of the slab list through height $H$, conditional on a cut there. At $H=0$ this law is concentrated on the empty list. We call $\mathcal B_H^\sigma$ the *exact-cut bridge law*.
- **lateral displacement entropy** ([source](#eq:lateral-entropy-bound)): Put $q=d-1$, and recall the iid lateral increments $S_i'\in\mathbb Z^q$ of the common blocks. Define $$h_j'=\mathscr H(S_1'+\cdots+S_j'),\qquad j\ge1.$$

      ],
      "exact_definition": "For a regeneration word $\\gamma$ with position sequence $(x_0,\\ldots,x_m)$, written relative to $x_0=0$, define its radius by $$\\mathcal R(\\gamma)=\\max_{0\\le j\\le m}|x_j|,$$ where $|\\cdot|$ denotes Euclidean norm.",
      "interpretation": "The largest Euclidean distance includes the terminal arrival.",
      "kind": "definition",
      "name": "spatial radius",
      "scope": "A regeneration word written relative to its starting site."
    },
    {
      "definition_anchor": "sec:bridges",
      "evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "sec:bridges",
          "exact_quote": "For integers $n\\ge0$, set $$u_n^\\sigma=P_0(T_n^\\sigma<T_{-1}^\\sigma),\n \\qquad F_\\sigma(n)=\\nu_\\sigma(L>n).\n \\label{eq:hitting-width-tail}$$",
          "source_id": "PM-220.1"
        }
      ],
      "exact_definition": "For integers $n\\ge0$, set $$u_n^\\sigma=P_0(T_n^\\sigma<T_{-1}^\\sigma),\n \\qquad F_\\sigma(n)=\\nu_\\sigma(L>n).\n \\label{eq:hitting-width-tail}$$",
      "interpretation": "The hitting probability and regeneration width tail are distinct quantities.",
      "kind": "definition",
      "name": "width tail",
      "scope": "Coordinate coexistence."
    },
    {

        {
          "anchor": "pm-h-19",
          "exact_quote": "A *contact* is a positive departure site at infinity distance at most $R$ from some negative departure site.",
          "source_id": "PM-220.1"
        }
      ],
      "exact_definition": "A *contact* is a positive departure site at infinity distance at most $R$ from some negative departure site.",
      "interpretation": "Contact counts proximity of departure sites regardless of marks.",
      "kind": "definition",
      "name": "contact",
      "scope": "The opposed tapes."
    },
    {
      "definition_anchor": "eq:contact-count",
      "evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "eq:contact-count",
          "exact_quote": "The quantity on which the two remaining estimates will disagree is $$m(J)=\\sum_{j=1}^J\\mathbf P\\bigl\\{\n  \\text{a contact occurs in a positive block of index in }\n                      (2^j,2^{j+1}]\\bigr\\},\\qquad J\\ge1.\n \\label{eq:contact-count}$$",
          "source_id": "PM-220.1"
        }
      ],
      "exact_definition": "The quantity on which the two remaining estimates will disagree is $$m(J)=\\sum_{j=1}^J\\mathbf P\\bigl\\{\n  \\text{a contact occurs in a positive block of index in }\n                      (2^j,2^{j+1}]\\bigr\\},\\qquad J\\ge1.\n \\label{eq:contact-count}$$",
      "interpretation": "This expectation counts dyadic block-index intervals containing contacts.",
      "kind": "definition",
      "name": "contact statistic",
      "scope": "Opposed tapes under coordinate coexistence."
    },
    {
      "definition_anchor": "pm-h-8",
      "evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "pm-h-8",
          "exact_quote": "To describe the pieces between cuts, call a finite marked word $\\gamma$ from zero a *regeneration word* if it has the following properties:\n\n1.  all its heights are nonnegative;\n\n2.  it ends at a candidate;\n\n3.  every earlier candidate in the word is undercut later within the word, meaning that a subsequent height is strictly smaller than that candidate’s height.",
          "source_id": "PM-220.1"
        }
      ],
      "exact_definition": "To describe the pieces between cuts, call a finite marked word $\\gamma$ from zero a *regeneration word* if it has the following properties:\n\n1.  all its heights are nonnegative;\n\n2.  it ends at a candidate;\n\n3.  every earlier candidate in the word is undercut later within the word, meaning that a subsequent height is strictly smaller than that candidate’s height.",
      "interpretation": "A regeneration word stays nonnegative, ends at a candidate, and undercuts every earlier candidate within the word.",
      "kind": "definition",
      "name": "regeneration word",
      "scope": "The conditioned directional walk."
    },
    {
      "definition_anchor": "pm-h-8",
      "evidence": [

          "anchor": "pm-h-8",
          "exact_quote": "Its *width* $L(\\gamma)$ is its terminal height.",
          "source_id": "PM-220.1"
        }
      ],
      "exact_definition": "Its *width* $L(\\gamma)$ is its terminal height.",
      "interpretation": "Width records spatial height rather than the elapsed number of steps.",
      "kind": "definition",
      "name": "word width",
      "scope": "Regeneration words in a normalized direction."
    },
    {

          "exact_quote": "A candidate time $t$ is a *cut* if $$h(X_n)\\ge h(X_t)\\qquad(n\\ge t).$$",
          "source_id": "PM-220.1"
        }
      ],
      "exact_definition": "A candidate time $t$ is a *cut* if $$h(X_n)\\ge h(X_t)\\qquad(n\\ge t).$$",
      "interpretation": "A cut requires the entire subsequent path to avoid lower heights.",
      "kind": "definition",
      "name": "cut",

    {
      "definition_anchor": "pm-h-18",

          "source_id": "PM-220.1"
        }
      ],
      "exact_definition": "If $\\bar u_H^\\sigma>0$, let $\\mathcal B_H^\\sigma$ denote the law of the slab list through height $H$, conditional on a cut there. At $H=0$ this law is concentrated on the empty list. We call $\\mathcal B_H^\\sigma$ the *exact-cut bridge law*.",
      "interpretation": "The bridge conditions a finite slab list on ending at a prescribed width renewal.",
      "kind": "definition",
      "name": "exact-cut bridge law",

          "source_id": "PM-220.1"

      "interpretation": "Entropy is taken for the accumulated lateral displacement of independent common blocks.",
      "kind": "definition",

Define $$\\tau=\\inf\\{i:T_k(Y)\\le i<T_N(Y),\\quad\n       \\|Y_i-\\beta_j\\|_\\infty\\le R\\text{ for some }j\\ge0\\},$$ with $\\inf\\varnothing=\\infty$. <a id="pm-s3"></a>
## 3. Proof roadmap

- **S1** [claim](#stmt-thm-main) / [proof](#proof-stmt-prop-contact-lower-2): Opposite-sign contradiction and coordinate reduction -> For every fixed nonzero direction, the annealed escape probability is either zero or one under the stated environment assumptions.
- **S2** [claim](#stmt-lem-separated-weights) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-separated-weights): Separated row sigma-fields and killed path enumeration -> A marked path reveals only its active departure rows. Separation of those rows permits exact factorization, including stopped and surviving path laws.
- **S3** [claim](#stmt-prop-cut-law) / [proof](#proof-stmt-prop-cut-law): Finite-prefix factorization and repeated script tests -> Conditioning on nonbacktracking produces independent regeneration words; the raw suffix law is identified by finite prefixes rather than a Markov property at a future-selected cut.
- **S4** [claim](#stmt-prop-sign-union) / [proof](#proof-stmt-prop-sign-union): Slab exit and regeneration concatenation -> Positive directional escape makes the two opposite signs exhaustive and implies positive nonbacktracking probability.
- **S5** [claim](#stmt-prop-mean-width) / [proof](#proof-stmt-prop-mean-width): Record renewal density and truncated strong laws -> Regeneration words have finite mean spatial width, without a time moment assumption.
- **S6** [claim](#stmt-lem-stationary-maximal) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-stationary-maximal): Disjoint interval covering and stationarity -> Stationarity and integrability control the largest initial running average through a maximal inequality.
- **S7** [claim](#stmt-prop-radius-moment) / [proof](#proof-stmt-prop-radius-moment): Tilted records, separated connectors and disjoint supremum windows -> Hypothetical coexistence forces finite mean spatial radius for both regeneration laws.
- **S8** [claim](#stmt-lem-opposite-rays) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-opposite-rays): Strong law, tail determinism and opposite-ray contradiction -> Under hypothetical coexistence, the two directional escape events have deterministic opposite limiting rays.
- **S9** [claim](#stmt-prop-coordinate-reduction) / [proof](#proof-stmt-prop-coordinate-reduction): Projection onto a coordinate with nonzero ray component -> An intermediate directional escape probability would imply coexistence in a coordinate direction.
- **S10** [claim](#stmt-prop-exact-cut-bridges) / [proof](#proof-stmt-prop-exact-cut-bridges): Normalized product weights and unique slab parsing -> Exact-cut bridges have normalized product weights and preserve their law when slab lists are reversed while each word is traversed forward.
- **S11** [claim](#stmt-lem-width-tail) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-width-tail): Renewal identity subtraction and dyadic tail summation -> Finite mean width yields a dyadically summable tail and controls differences of hitting probabilities.
- **S12** [claim](#stmt-prop-common-blocks) / [proof](#proof-stmt-prop-common-blocks): Intersection renewal recursion and radius summation -> Independent opposed renewal tapes have independent common blocks with finite mean width and integrable sums of slab radii.
- **S13** [claim](#stmt-prop-entropy-comparison) / [proof](#proof-stmt-prop-entropy-comparison): Conditional mutual information and displacement entropy -> The relative entropy cost of the actual chunk placement is controlled by the growth of lateral-displacement entropy.
- **S14** [claim](#stmt-prop-reference-contact) / [proof](#proof-stmt-prop-reference-contact): First proximity arrival and shared-environment exit probabilities -> Independent bridge reference laws bound middle-chunk proximity contacts by the width tails.
- **S15** [claim](#stmt-prop-contact-upper) / [proof](#proof-stmt-prop-contact-upper): Binary relative entropy and telescoping over dyadic scales -> Entropy comparison gives an upper bound on the number of dyadic block-index intervals containing contacts under hypothetical coordinate coexistence.
- **S16** [claim](#stmt-lem-posterior-max) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-posterior-max): Scalar martingale maxima and threshold crossing -> A finite family of nonnegative martingales has an exponentially integrable sum of running maxima on a logarithmic scale, including bounds restricted to arbitrary events.
- **S17** [claim](#stmt-prop-first-contact) / [proof](#proof-stmt-prop-first-contact): Stopped posterior marginal transfer and exit-probability bins -> A scripted prefix and endpoint posterior estimates bound the probability of a late first proximity contact.
- **S18** [claim](#stmt-prop-contact-lower) / [proof](#proof-stmt-prop-contact-lower): Cut averaging and conversion of depth labels to block indices -> Under hypothetical coordinate coexistence, first-contact estimates force a lower contact count incompatible with the entropy upper bound.

With the convention that distance from an empty set is infinite, suppose that $$\\mathop{\\mathrm{dist}}_\\infty(\\operatorname{Act}(\\gamma),B)>R.$$ If $f\\ge0$ is measurable with respect to the rows in $B$, then $$\\label{eq:separated-weights}\n E_Q[p_\\omega(\\gamma)f(\\omega)]\n =W(\\gamma)E_Q[f(\\omega)].$$ The conclusion also holds when the active set is empty, without a separation requirement.*\n\n*For a deterministic set $F\\subseteq\\mathbb Z^d$, the quenched law of the marked walk stopped on its first arrival in $F$, including the arrival site, depends only on rows outside $F$. In particular, the quenched probability of any measurable infinite-path event that implies the walk never enters $F$ depends only on those rows. Such probabilities may therefore be used as $f$ in <a href=\"#eq:separated-weights\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:separated-weights\">[eq:separated-weights]</a> whenever the exterior rows are separated from $\\operatorname{Act}(\\gamma)$.",

<a id="pm-s4"></a>
## 4. Engineering Triptych

### Panel 1 - epistemic contract

- **directional_escape**: The annealed probability of directional escape is either zero or one.
- **marked_marginal**: Summing over the transition marks preserves the original walk law.
- **separated_weights**: The expectation of a marked prefix weight times a separated row-measurable function factors into the product of expectations.
- **regeneration_words**: Relative marked words between successive cut boundaries are independent and identically distributed under the nonbacktracking conditioned law.

### Panel 2 - negative knowledge

- **mixing_substitution**: Apply the directional escape conclusion using decay of correlations alone. -> Correlation decay does not supply the separated row independence used in the proof.
- **quenched_selection**: Read the theorem as identifying the escape sign in a given environment. -> The conclusion concerns the environment-averaged escape probability and supplies no procedure for selecting an escape sign.
- **future_cut_markov**: Use an ordinary stopping-time Markov argument at a regeneration cut. -> A cut is selected using the future, so this shortcut does not justify its conditional suffix law.
- **spatial_to_temporal**: Convert finite spatial width into finite regeneration time or positive speed. -> The spatial estimate supplies no time moment and does not determine velocity.
- **finite_escape_certificate**: Certify directional escape from a finite observed trajectory. -> Escape is an infinite-path event, and candidate cuts require a subsequent path that never undercuts their endpoint.

### Panel 3 - reference implementation and oracle

Applicability: `not_applicable`. The result concerns directional escape of an infinite walk in a random environment. Regeneration cuts depend on an infinite continuation that never undercuts its endpoint, and the proof excludes coexistence through asymptotic contact bounds. These constructions do not supply a finite algorithm that decides escape from finite path data.

<a id="pm-s6"></a>
## 6. Applicability limits and search

- **L1** (hypothesis): The theorem concerns nearest-neighbor lattice walks in the stated dimension regime. Blocked use: ['Applying the result to arbitrary state spaces, long-range steps, or a different dimension regime.']
- **L2** (hypothesis): Uniform ellipticity requires a deterministic positive lower bound shared by every transition row. Blocked use: ['Replacing uniform ellipticity with mere positivity in the dependent setting.']
- **L3** (hypothesis): Finite-range independence applies to full transition-row sigma-fields for separated deterministic sets, including infinite sets. Blocked use: ['Substituting pairwise decorrelation or independence of only finite samples.']
- **L4** (edge_case): The direction is fixed before probability is taken and may be irrational. Blocked use: ['Choosing the direction from the realized environment or trajectory and invoking the fixed-direction theorem.']
- **L5** (counterexample_scope): Decay of local correlations is weaker than the exact independence assumption used here. Blocked use: ['Extending the theorem to polynomially mixing environments solely from correlation decay.']
- **L6** (nonconstructive): Regeneration cuts depend on the future; the construction does not supply a finite-prefix decision procedure for directional escape. Blocked use: ['Certifying infinite escape or identifying a cut from a finite observed trajectory.']
- **L7** (edge_case): Finite mean spatial width supplies no moment bound for the time spent in a regeneration word. Blocked use: ['Inferring bounded runtime or positive speed from spatial integrability.']
- **L8** (hypothesis): Disjoint paths may still reveal correlated rows; separation must exceed the dependence range. Blocked use: ['Treating disjoint visited sets as independent components.']
- **L9** (edge_case): Bridge symmetry reverses the slab list while preserving the steps within every word. Blocked use: ['Reversing each path word and claiming the same bridge law.']
- **L10** (edge_case): The transferred posterior functions retain their original conditional meaning; only the stopped path marginal is transferred. Blocked use: ['Interpreting them as posteriors conditioned on a freshly sampled environment or extending the transferred law beyond its stopping boundary.']
- **L11** (unknown): An effective procedure determining the escape sign for an arbitrary admissible environment law is ABSENT. Blocked use: ['Claiming the qualitative directional law computes which escape sign occurs.']

<a id="pm-s7"></a>
## 7. Complete mathematical paper


We prove a directional zero–one law for uniformly elliptic nearest-neighbor random walks in stationary, ergodic, finite-range-dependent environments on ℤ<sup><i>d</i></sup>, <i>d</i> ≥ 3. For every fixed nonzero real direction, the probability of escape in that direction, averaged over the environment, is either zero or one. Finite-range dependence is imposed on the full transition rows: collections of rows at distance greater than a fixed range are independent, including collections indexed by infinite deterministic sets.

<a id="pm-h-1"></a>

| [stmt-prop-coordinate-reduction](#stmt-prop-coordinate-reduction) | proposition | `prop:coordinate-reduction` | 9 | If $0<P_0(A_v)<1$ for some fixed nonzero real direction $v$, then there is a coordinate $i\in\{1,\ldots,d\}$ such that $$P_0(A_{e_i})>0\qquad\text{and}\qquad P_0(A_{-e_i})>0.$$* |
| [stmt-prop-exact-cut-bridges](#stmt-prop-exact-cut-bridges) | proposition | `prop:exact-cut-bridges` | 10 | The renewal masses satisfy |

| [stmt-prop-first-contact](#stmt-prop-first-contact) | proposition | `prop:first-contact` | 17 | Assume $p_+p_->0$, and let $M\le k<r<N$ be integers with $r-k\ge R$. |
| [stmt-prop-contact-lower](#stmt-prop-contact-lower) | proposition | `prop:contact-lower` | 18 | Suppose that $P_0(A_{e_1})>0$ and $P_0(A_{-e_1})>0$, and let $m(J)$ be the contact statistic of the opposed tapes defined in [eq:contact-count]. |

<a id="sec:introduction"></a>
### Introduction

A random walk in a random environment encounters the same transition probabilities whenever it returns to a site. This persistence distinguishes the model from a walk whose transition probabilities are resampled at each step. Directional transience asks whether the walk eventually moves beyond every hyperplane perpendicular to a fixed direction. The directional zero–one question is whether this escape event can have probability strictly between zero and one after averaging over the environment.

<a id="pm-h-3"></a>
#### Model and main theorem

Fix $d\ge3$ and let $U=\{\pm e_1,\ldots,\pm e_d\}$ be the coordinate unit steps. Set $$\Delta_U=\left\{p\in[0,1]^U:\sum_{e\in U}p(e)=1\right\},
 \qquad \Omega=\Delta_U^{\mathbb Z^d},$$ with its product Borel sigma-field. An environment $\omega\in\Omega$ assigns the row $\omega(x,\cdot)$ to each site $x$. Let $Q$ be a Borel probability law on $\Omega$ satisfying the following assumptions.

1.  *Stationarity and ergodicity.* For $z\in\mathbb Z^d$, define $(\theta_z\omega)(x,e)=\omega(x+z,e)$. The law $Q$ is invariant under every $\theta_z$, and every event invariant modulo null sets under all these translations has probability zero or one.

2.  *Uniform ellipticity.* For some deterministic $\kappa>0$, $$Q\bigl(\omega(x,e)\ge\kappa
              \text{ for every }x\in\mathbb Z^d,\ e\in U\bigr)=1.$$

<a id="eq:finite-range"></a>
3.  *Finite-range dependence.* For some deterministic integer $R\ge0$, the sigma-fields $$\mathcal F_A=\sigma\bigl(\omega(x,e):x\in A,\ e\in U\bigr),
     \qquad \mathcal F_B=\sigma\bigl(\omega(x,e):x\in B,\ e\in U\bigr)$$ are independent whenever the nonempty deterministic sets $A,B\subseteq\mathbb Z^d$ satisfy $$\label{eq:finite-range}
     \mathop{\mathrm{dist}}_\infty(A,B):=
     \inf_{a\in A,\,b\in B}\lVert a-b\rVert_\infty>R.$$ This condition includes infinite sets.

For fixed $\omega$, let $P_{x,\omega}$ be the law of the Markov chain started at $x$ with transitions $$P_{x,\omega}(X_{n+1}=y+e\mid X_n=y)=\omega(y,e).$$ Its annealed law is $P_x=\int P_{x,\omega}\,Q(d\omega)$. For each fixed $\ell\in\mathbb R^d\setminus\{0\}$, write $$A_\ell=\{X_n\cdot\ell\longrightarrow+\infty\}.$$

<a id="stmt-thm-main"></a>
<div id="thm:main" class="theorem">

**Theorem 1** (Directional zero–one law). *Under assumptions <span class="upright">(i)–(iii)</span>, for every fixed $\ell\in\mathbb R^d\setminus\{0\}$, $$P_0(A_\ell)\in\{0,1\}.$$*

</div>

The dependence range and ellipticity constant may vary with the law $Q$. The direction may be irrational; it is fixed before taking probability. The proof uses stationarity and finite-range independence directly, without an additional appeal to ergodicity. Its independence arguments concern separated sigma-fields of rows, rather than any representation of the environment in terms of independent labels.

<a id="pm-h-4"></a>
#### Background and relation to earlier work

Directional zero–one laws are a basic part of the transience theory of random walks in random environments. Kalikow’s work <a id="pm-cite-1"></a>[[Kalikow1981](#ref-Kalikow1981)], together with the regeneration formulation of Sznitman and Zerner <a id="pm-cite-2"></a>[[SznitmanZerner1999](#ref-SznitmanZerner1999), Lemma 1.1], established the sign-union law for uniformly elliptic iid environments, $$P_0(A_\ell\cup A_{-\ell})\in\{0,1\}.$$ This leaves open which sign is chosen when the union has probability one. In two dimensions, Zerner and Merkl <a id="pm-cite-3"></a>[[ZernerMerkl2001](#ref-ZernerMerkl2001)] proved the full directional zero–one law for iid elliptic environments; Zerner <a id="pm-cite-4"></a>[[Zerner2007](#ref-Zerner2007)] subsequently gave a revised proof. The planar arguments exploit intersections of paths. The question in higher dimensions requires a different way to exclude two possible escape signs.

Regeneration is a central tool for this problem and for laws of large numbers. Sznitman and Zerner <a id="pm-cite-5"></a>[[SznitmanZerner1999](#ref-SznitmanZerner1999), Theorem 1.4 and Corollary 1.5] constructed independent increments after suitable record times in an iid environment. Results on asymptotic directions further distinguish a deterministic axis from the choice of a sign on that axis: see Simenhaus <a id="pm-cite-6"></a>[[Simenhaus2007](#ref-Simenhaus2007)] and Drewitz and Ramírez <a id="pm-cite-7"></a>[[DrewitzRamirez2010](#ref-DrewitzRamirez2010)]. A limiting direction or a conditional law of large numbers does not by itself establish a directional zero–one law.

For dependent environments, Comets and Zeitouni <a id="pm-cite-8"></a>[[CometsZeitouni2004](#ref-CometsZeitouni2004)] used environment-independent forced steps to construct approximate regeneration under mixing assumptions, with additional hypotheses yielding a law of large numbers. That forcing device is an antecedent of the marked scripts used here. Rassoul-Agha <a id="pm-cite-9"></a>[[RassoulAgha2005](#ref-RassoulAgha2005)] derived a law of large numbers under Gibbs mixing assumptions and a directional zero–one hypothesis. Guo <a id="pm-cite-10"></a>[[Guo2014](#ref-Guo2014)] proved conditional velocity results under a mixing condition that includes finite-range-dependent environments. These conclusions concern velocity and do not determine the probabilities of the two directional escape events. Exact finite-range independence is also stronger than decay of local correlations: Bramson, Zeitouni, and Zerner <a id="pm-cite-11"></a>[[BramsonZeitouniZerner2006](#ref-BramsonZeitouniZerner2006), Theorem 3] constructed stationary, uniformly elliptic, polynomially mixing environments in dimension at least three with positive probability of escape in each of two opposite directions.

The companion article <a id="pm-cite-12"></a>[[OpenAI2026](#ref-OpenAI2026), Theorem 1.1] proves the iid directional zero–one law under strict ellipticity: every transition probability is positive, but no uniform lower bound is required. Here we extend the uniformly elliptic case to finite-range dependence. The proof adapts the radius-and-contact strategy developed in <a id="pm-cite-13"></a>[[OpenAI2026](#ref-OpenAI2026), Sections 2–7]. In that strategy, coexistence first forces integrability of spatial regeneration radii. Two independent sequences of regeneration pieces are then placed in an opposed arrangement, and entropy and endpoint-posterior estimates give incompatible bounds on their contacts. We retain this architecture and prove all required estimates for the present environment law. The finite-range extension depends on keeping track of the rows actually used by a marked path, replacing intersections by proximity within the dependence range, and transferring only the stopped path laws justified by separated rows. No conditional density assumption, independent-label representation, positive speed, or moment bound on regeneration times is imposed.

<a id="pm-h-5"></a>
#### Proof strategy and additional ingredients

The argument rules out coexistence of escape in opposite directions. For a fixed direction, we construct independent regeneration pieces: finite path segments ending at new record heights that the subsequent path never undercuts. This construction proves that positive probability of escape in that direction makes the two escape signs exhaustive.

Finite-range dependence creates two obstacles to the usual iid reasoning. Disjoint paths can use correlated rows, and an observed path can change the law of nearby unobserved rows. We split each transition into two marked choices of constant weight and a remaining choice. A prescribed sequence of the constant-weight choices moves beyond the dependence range without using any further row information. This leaves a region of genuinely independent rows for a continuation that stays beyond its endpoint. The prescribed sequences cannot overlap, so the regeneration pieces have an unambiguous concatenation law. Conditioning at their endpoints is proved by enumeration of finite prefixes, since those endpoints are selected using the future.

Under coexistence, the regeneration pieces have finite mean spatial radius. The proof uses a large transverse excursion to splice in an oppositely directed continuation, separated from the observed path by a tilted hyperplane and a short marked connector. Repeating the construction would place uniformly positive probability in disjoint windows for the walk’s final directional supremum. This contradiction gives the radius bound. It yields opposite deterministic limiting rays and reduces an arbitrary real direction to a coordinate direction.

For that coordinate, place independent positive and negative sequences of regeneration pieces in an opposed arrangement. A contact means that their departure sites come within distance $R$. Common regeneration heights divide the arrangement into iid pairs of lists. Finite spatial moments bound the entropy of their cumulative lateral displacement. Comparing long chunks with independent bridges—concatenated regeneration pieces conditioned on prescribed total widths—then shows that among the first $J$ dyadic intervals of block indices, the expected number containing a contact is $O(J/\log J)$.

The opposite estimate uses endpoint alignment. Start a negative path, conditioned to stay below its starting height, just below a positive bridge’s endpoint, and search for their first contact along the positive path. Posterior probabilities for a finite set of possible lateral endpoints form a vector of martingales. The sum of their running maxima has an exponential tail on the scale of the logarithm of the number of endpoints. The marked segment ending an observed prefix at a lower height permits comparison with a fresh path law up to its first exit from a specified slab. The posterior functions retain their original meaning; only the stopped path marginal is transferred. Combining this estimate with nearby-point comparisons of quenched exit probabilities forces $\Omega(J/\log\log J)$ contact scales, contradicting the entropy bound.

The finite-range adaptations are the marked separation, killing on entry to neighborhoods of radius $R$, and comparison of stopped posterior processes across the resulting gaps between active rows. We prove in full the vector-martingale estimate of <a id="pm-cite-14"></a>[[OpenAI2026](#ref-OpenAI2026), Lemma 6.1]; it controls the cost of selecting a path through an endpoint constraint.

Section <a href="#sec:cuts" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:cuts">2</a> establishes regeneration and mean widths. Section <a href="#sec:radius" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:radius">3</a> proves spatial integrability and reduces to coordinate coexistence. Sections <a href="#sec:bridges" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:bridges">4</a> and <a href="#sec:entropy" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:entropy">5</a> construct the opposed arrangement and its contact upper bound. Section <a href="#sec:posteriors" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:posteriors">6</a> proves the endpoint estimate, and Section <a href="#sec:contact-lower" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:contact-lower">7</a> obtains the contradictory lower bound.

<a id="sec:cuts"></a>
### Separated rows and regeneration words

We first construct independent successive pieces of a walk that stays above its initial level in a prescribed direction. Finite-range dependence requires a gap between the rows used by consecutive pieces. We create that gap by marking some transitions whose probabilities are constant, so that the walk can cross the gap without using its environmental rows. The construction will also prove the directional sign-union statement and a finite mean for the spatial width of each piece. Throughout this section the direction may be any fixed nonzero real vector. We adapt the regeneration and record-count arguments of <a id="pm-cite-15"></a>[[OpenAI2026](#ref-OpenAI2026), Section 2], proving here the separated-row identities needed for finite-range dependence.

<a id="pm-h-7"></a>
#### Marks and the rows a path uses

We use the same splitting device as the independent-coin construction of Comets and Zeitouni <a id="pm-cite-16"></a>[[CometsZeitouni2004](#ref-CometsZeitouni2004), Equation (2.1)]: an environment-independent choice forces a step, and a residual choice restores the prescribed transition row. Two distinguished marks will also prevent overlapping occurrences of the prescribed step sequence introduced below.

<a id="eq:marked-kernel"></a>
Fix $\lambda>0$ with $2\lambda<\kappa$. Enlarge the walk by giving each step a mark in $\{0,1,*\}$. Conditional on the environment and the marked past, a step from $x$ has joint probabilities $$\label{eq:marked-kernel}
 \begin{aligned}
 P_{x,\omega}(X_1=x+e,\text{ mark }0)&=\lambda,\\
 P_{x,\omega}(X_1=x+e,\text{ mark }1)&=\lambda,\\
 P_{x,\omega}(X_1=x+e,\text{ mark }*)&=\omega(x,e)-2\lambda,
 \qquad e\in U.
 \end{aligned}$$ These probabilities are nonnegative and sum to one. Summing over the marks recovers the original transition row, so every assertion about the unmarked path is unchanged. We keep the notation $P_{x,\omega}$ and $P_x$ for the marked quenched and annealed laws. We call these laws *raw* when no condition of staying on one side of a barrier is imposed. The path filtration includes both positions and the marks of steps already taken. All constructions take place on the full-measure set where the ellipticity inequalities hold at every site; the marked kernel may be defined arbitrarily elsewhere.

A finite marked word $\gamma$ consists of positions $x_0,\ldots,x_t$, with $x_{j+1}-x_j\in U$, and one mark for each of its $t$ steps. Its departure set and active departure set are $$\mathop{\mathrm{Dep}}(\gamma)=\{x_0,\ldots,x_{t-1}\},\qquad
 \operatorname{Act}(\gamma)
 =\{x_j:0\le j<t,\ \text{step }j\text{ has mark }*\}.$$ Let $p_\omega(\gamma)$ be the product of the marked transition probabilities in <a href="#eq:marked-kernel" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:marked-kernel">[eq:marked-kernel]</a>, and put $$W(\gamma)=E_Q[p_\omega(\gamma)].$$ Thus $W(\gamma)$ is the annealed probability of the specified prefix. Stationarity makes it invariant under translation of all positions in the word. The terminal arrival uses no row. More generally, a visited row affects $p_\omega(\gamma)$ only if it is an active departure.

<a id="stmt-lem-separated-weights"></a>
<div id="lem:separated-weights" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:separated-weights"></a>
**Lemma 2** (Separated path weights). *Let $\gamma$ be a fixed finite marked word, and let $B\subseteq\mathbb Z^d$ be a deterministic set. With the convention that distance from an empty set is infinite, suppose that $$\mathop{\mathrm{dist}}_\infty(\operatorname{Act}(\gamma),B)>R.$$ If $f\ge0$ is measurable with respect to the rows in $B$, then $$\label{eq:separated-weights}
 E_Q[p_\omega(\gamma)f(\omega)]
 =W(\gamma)E_Q[f(\omega)].$$ The conclusion also holds when the active set is empty, without a separation requirement.*

*For a deterministic set $F\subseteq\mathbb Z^d$, the quenched law of the marked walk stopped on its first arrival in $F$, including the arrival site, depends only on rows outside $F$. In particular, the quenched probability of any measurable infinite-path event that implies the walk never enters $F$ depends only on those rows. Such probabilities may therefore be used as $f$ in <a href="#eq:separated-weights" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:separated-weights">[eq:separated-weights]</a> whenever the exterior rows are separated from $\operatorname{Act}(\gamma)$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-separated-weights"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-separated-weights</summary>

*Proof.* Factors with marks $0$ or $1$ are constant. All other factors in $p_\omega(\gamma)$ are functions of rows in $\operatorname{Act}(\gamma)$, with repeated departures from one row retaining that same row. The finite-range assumption <a href="#eq:finite-range" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:finite-range">[eq:finite-range]</a> gives independence from the rows in $B$, also when $B$ is infinite. This proves <a href="#eq:separated-weights" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:separated-weights">[eq:separated-weights]</a>; if the active set is empty, $p_\omega(\gamma)$ itself is constant.

Before the first arrival in $F$, every transition departs from its complement. Prefix probabilities for the stopped walk therefore use only exterior rows, including when the prefix ends with that arrival. These prefixes determine the stopped path law, so all its measurable event probabilities have the same row dependence. Equivalently, one may kill the walk on arrival in $F$. An infinite-path event implying avoidance of $F$ is determined by this killed trajectory: it is false on killed paths and retains its original meaning on surviving paths. This proves the last assertion. ◻

</details>

We will always fix the relevant finite words before applying this lemma. The separated sets are then deterministic. For example, two prescribed words with departure sets at distance greater than $R$ have the same joint weight in independent environments as in a common environment with conditionally independent walks. The lemma also applies to an avoidance event involving an entire infinite continuation.

<a id="pm-h-8"></a>
#### Cuts with independent continuations

Fix a direction $v\ne0$, rescale it so that $\lVert v\rVert_\infty=1$, and write $h(x)=v\cdot x$. Choose a coordinate step $a_v\in U$ with $h(a_v)=1$. Fix an integer $M>dR+1$, the same integer for every direction considered, and set $\xi=\lambda^M$. The prescribed *script* in direction $v$ consists of $M$ steps $a_v$, the first with mark $0$ and all the others with mark $1$. Two occurrences of the script cannot overlap in a step: the initial $0$ of a second occurrence would have to coincide with a $1$ of the first.

We call time zero a record. A positive time is an *ordinary record* if its height strictly exceeds all preceding heights. A script starting at a record has a *candidate* at its end. A candidate time $t$ is a *cut* if $$h(X_n)\ge h(X_t)\qquad(n\ge t).$$ Each candidate is itself a strict record. These definitions initially refer to the whole path starting at time zero. For a walk started elsewhere we use heights relative to its starting site. Write $$D_v=\{h(X_n)\ge0\text{ for every }n\ge0\},\qquad
 p_v=P_0(D_v),\qquad
 \widehat P_v=P_0(\,\cdot\mid D_v)\quad(p_v>0).$$

The script separates the rows of a record prefix from those of a continuation above its endpoint; Figure <a href="#fig:cut-gap" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="fig:cut-gap">1</a> displays this distinction between visited and active sites. Indeed, for all $x,y\in\mathbb Z^d$, <a id="eq:height-distance"></a>$$\label{eq:height-distance}
 |h(x)-h(y)|\le d\lVert x-y\rVert_\infty.$$ If a record prefix ends at height $r$, every departure in that prefix has height strictly below $r$. The script has no active departures, whereas a continuation staying above its endpoint uses only rows of height at least $r+M$. The required separation follows from $M>dR$.

<figure id="fig:cut-gap">

<figcaption>The gap at a candidate, shown schematically in height coordinates. Filled points indicate active prefix departures; orange arrows are the constant-weight script steps. The height gap separates every active prefix row from every permitted continuation row by more than $R$ in $ℓ^{∞}$-distance, although the script itself visits the intervening sites.</figcaption>
</figure>

<a id="pm-figsrc-fig-cut-gap"></a>
